TSM — Evidence appendix
All harvested proof points · company first, then peers

Evidence

Claims by section
business-model
  • TSMC manufactures integrated circuits to other companies' designs. It owns no product line of its own, sells no chip under its own brand, and competes for the manufacturing step between a customer's finished design and a packaged, tested die.
  • Price per wafer is set node by node in bilateral negotiation, with no published price book, no indexation and no take-or-pay.
  • That five-year life sets the margin lifecycle of every node: a ramping node runs at a low margin and dilutes the consolidated figure, a node in its fourth year reaches the corporate average (management expects N3 to cross it in the second half of 2026, per the Q1 2026 call), and a node past five years runs at a margin the CFO describes as "generally very high" (Q1 2026 call).
  • Realized incremental operating margin over the full 2019-2025 cycle is 57.1%, roughly six points above the 50.8% average margin, so operating leverage is real but not extreme, because the fixed-cost base rebuilds itself every five years through capex.
  • Given the Q2 2026 wafer mix (N2 3%, N3 30%, N5 33%, N7 11%, mature 23%) and a 67.7% consolidated gross margin, the fully-depreciated N5/N7 band has to run somewhere in the 73-85% range across every plausible assumption for the other bands.
  • The purchase decision is made two to three years before the wafer ships: "as process technology complexity increases, the engagement lead time with customers is now at least 2 to 3 years in advance" (CEO, Q4 2025 call).
  • Three of the four things management names as drivers appeared. Node-mix migration explains 94-110% of wafer-revenue growth. AI-driven demand appeared, but at roughly double the disclosed "high teens percent" once the non-accelerator HPC residual is included, and it is one exposure, not two. Volume did not appear until H1 2026. Pricing power did not appear as a margin driver, on management's own testimony.
competitive-advantage
  • The leading-edge franchise holds a structural advantage of the strongest class available in manufacturing, and it is expensive to hold. The mature franchise earns excess returns today and is open to erosion. The backend franchise earns excess returns today and is the one part of the company where rivals are actively attacking the position.
  • TSMC took 72.3% of merchant foundry revenue in Q1 2026, up from 70.4% the quarter before [web], and a bottom-up build of merchant sub-7nm revenue from each player's own disclosure puts its share of that market between 77% and 93% depending on how much of Samsung's and SMIC's revenue is assumed to be leading-edge (Section 4).
  • The two attackers with the balance sheets to try are both losing money doing it: Intel Foundry lost US$10,318m on US$17,826m of segment revenue in FY2025, a −57.9% operating margin, of which only US$307m came from external customers (INTC 10-K FY2025); Samsung's combined foundry and System LSI operating loss is estimated at KRW 6tn in 2025 [web], and its foundry revenue declined 5.8% quarter over quarter in Q1 2026 while TSMC's rose 6.3%.
  • The margin understates the moat, because the margin is a policy choice, not a market-clearing outcome.
  • The franchise is the largest mature-node foundry business in the world, about 3.5x UMC's entire company, 2.9x SMIC's and 4.0x GlobalFoundries', sitting on a largely depreciated asset base and selling qualification-locked specialty processes.
  • Peer gross margins were 29.0% (UMC), 24.9% (GlobalFoundries), 23.2% (Tower), 21.0% (SMIC) and 11.8% (Hua Hong) in FY2025, and the residual arithmetic bounds TSMC's mature franchise at roughly 45-55%, 16 to 43 points above every one of them.
  • But it compounded at 2.0% a year in NT$ from 2018 to 2025 while the company compounded at 20.5%, and at 2.9% a year in US dollars from 2020 to 2025 against SMIC's 19.0% and Hua Hong's 20.1%.
  • TSMC has also deliberately lowered the switching barriers around its own flows: it contributed 3Dblox to the IEEE, qualified Amkor, ASE, Integrated Service Technology and SK hynix as 3DFabric partners, and signed a ten-year agreement in October 2024 to contract turnkey advanced packaging and test from Amkor's Arizona facility [web].
  • Developing one node and equipping a fab to run it costs on the order of US$20-30bn before a wafer ships, and A14 alone takes five to seven years from development to volume production.
  • Depreciated 200mm and older 300mm equipment is available on the secondary market, process recipes at 28nm and above are ten to twenty years old and widely diffused, and the binding leading-edge input does not bind here: 28nm and above use DUV immersion, which ASML, Nikon and Canon all supply and which China can import.
  • Every major government subsidises a rival: the US CHIPS Act, the European Chips Act (up to €5bn to ESMC), Japan's METI support for JASM and Rapidus, Korean support for Samsung. TSMC's own risk factor complains about unequal access to incentives. The consequence is quantified: overseas fabs dilute gross margin 2-3 percentage points in early stages, widening to 3-4 points later. This is the one moat dimension being actively eroded, and policy, not competition, is eroding it.
  • The worst year in the decade was 2019 at 46.0% gross margin, 34.8% operating margin and 28.6% ROIC, at the bottom of a smartphone demand trough with 7nm just ramping and capex at 43.0% of revenue. A business whose cyclical trough is a 29% ROIC is not competing away its returns.
  • Running FY2025 returns on invested capital including and excluding that pool gives 24.9% and 48.7% respectively. The operating business earns roughly 49%; the reported group earns roughly 25%. Half the reported return dilution is a treasury decision, not an industrial one.
  • There is no dimension on which the leading-edge gap is narrowing. The mature franchise is the opposite: the margin gap holds at a high level while the scale gap closes.
  • Against the three reference points, TSMC's leading-edge position is the first: a structural advantage that neither Intel Foundry nor Samsung has narrowed on any dimension in the table above, supporting pricing power the company chooses not to fully exercise and throwing off returns several multiples of any plausible cost of capital.
  • No US-listed company reproduces the combination of monopoly share, 50%+ ROIC and 33%-of-revenue capital intensity. ASML is the closest single name, with the caveat that its protection is patent- and physics-based while TSMC's is volume- and learning-based, and volume can in principle be bought.
competitive-landscape
  • The same corporate entity operates in two industries with opposite structures. At 7nm and below the Herfindahl index on merchant revenue is roughly 8,700 out of 10,000, more than three times the threshold US merger guidelines treat as highly concentrated. At 16nm and above it is roughly 2,000, which is the middle of the moderately-concentrated band.
  • Assuming every dollar of Samsung's, SMIC's and Intel Foundry's external revenue is leading-edge gives TSMC 77.2% of merchant sub-7nm revenue. On a realistic node split (Samsung's leading-edge portion at the midpoint of its band, SMIC's at US$850m) it is 93.4%. No assumption inside the plausible range moves it below three-quarters.
  • TSMC does not trail on any leading-edge metric checked. The two places a rival holds an advantage are outside the franchise: Samsung's memory business earns a higher gross margin than TSMC's foundry, a fact TSMC's own chief executive volunteered, and Intel holds High-NA tools TSMC says it does not require through 2029.
  • Not TSMC, historically. UMC shipped 3,195k, then 3,446k, then 3,870k 12-inch equivalents across 2023-2025 while its ASP declined 5.0% and then 5.4%; GlobalFoundries shipped 10.4% more wafers in 2025 at 10.4% lower prices.
  • The AI sub-pool is the structure that matters. TrendForce puts total 2026 industry 2.5D capacity at roughly 200,000 wafers per month, of which TSMC is 120,000-140,000 and OSAT partners 50,000-60,000 [web], so **TSMC is roughly 60-70% of 2.5D capacity**
  • The most recent quarter changes the shape of that comparison. On a full-year basis TSMC's backend line grew 41% while ASE's ATM grew 20% and Amkor 6%. On the most recent quarter the OSATs grew 28-30% against TSMC consolidated at 34-36%, and Amkor's growth rate quadrupled. The gap is closing because TSMC is outsourcing more, not because the OSATs won business away from it.
  • Where the profit pool sits, on a 45-55% estimated TSMC packaging gross margin: TSMC earns roughly US$4,400-5,400m of gross profit from advanced packaging against ASE's US$2,885m across its whole packaging-and-test operation and Amkor's US$939m — roughly as much as both combined, on about half their combined revenue.
  • at 45-55% against a 59.9% FY2025 corporate gross margin, a rising packaging mix pulls the consolidated number down, and management has never listed packaging mix among the six gross-margin factors it does name.
critical-points
  • Under the Q2 2026 wafer mix, reproducing a 67.7% consolidated gross margin requires the N5/N7 band, both past the five-year machinery life, to run at 73-85%. Roughly 44% of wafer revenue is currently in that fully-depreciated band, down from 50% in FY2025 as N2 and N3 take share. That is a mechanical headwind nobody is modelling as one.
  • AI-data-centre-driven demand is plausibly 35-45% of total revenue and supplied roughly three-quarters of 2023-2025 growth. The diversification implied by "AI is only high-teens percent of revenue" is not there.
expectations
  • Consensus is below the company on the current year and far above the historical distribution on the years after it. Management guides FY2026 US$ revenue growth to "slightly above 40%" while the sum of first-half actuals and quarterly consensus implies +38.9%; three years out, consensus asks TSMC to compound revenue at 32.0% a year from a US$122,556m base, and no company at or above US$100bn of revenue has achieved 30% for three years in the 403 observations available since 1990.
financial-history
  • Trailing four quarters to Q2 2026 gross margin is 64.2% and operating margin 56.1% against FY2025's 59.9% and 50.8%; the June quarter alone printed 67.7% and 60.3%.
forward-model
  • The standing target set on the Q4 2025 call is a US$ revenue compound rate approaching 25% for 2024-2029 with gross margin of "56% and higher through the cycle."
management-claims
  • Two implications follow, both against the framing: the moat is stronger than the reported margin because the margin is capped by policy, not by competition, and the downside case in which TSMC "loses pricing power" is less dangerous than it appears because a large cushion sits between what TSMC charges and what the market would pay.
  • Anyone still applying the 2023 claim forward overstates this franchise's margin by roughly 15-20 points.
  • "56% and higher through the cycle" was set on 15 January 2026 alongside a printed 62.3% and was 1,050 basis points below the run rate within two quarters.
  • Every published long-term target has been raised after the realized number passed it, in every instance: revenue compound rate 5-10% (2018) to 10-15% (2021) to about 20% (Jan 2025) to about 25% (Jan 2026); gross margin 50% to 53% to 56%; return on equity above 25% to high-20s. Target-setting here is a lagging indicator of the company's own results, and the targets are a floor management is willing to be held to, not a central case.
secular-trends
  • Between the 2022 revenue peak and FY2025, TSMC shipped fewer wafers and earned 68% more revenue. Shipments went from 15,253 thousand 12-inch equivalents in 2022 to 15,022 thousand in 2025, a decline of 1.5%, while net revenue went from NT$2,263,891m to NT$3,809,054m. All of the three-year growth came from realized price and product mix; volume subtracted from it.
  • TSMC's mature and specialty franchise performed like the mature-node cohort, not better than it. Every unit of outperformance is at the leading edge, where the competitive set is Samsung Foundry and Intel Foundry, and where Intel Foundry lost US$10.3bn in 2025.
  • Among the three foundries that disclose 12-inch-equivalent wafer volume, TSMC's share of shipments rose from 70.0% to 70.9% in 2025, 0.9 points, while its revenue share on the TrendForce measure rose 5.5 points.
  • The one indicator that is not at an extreme is capital intensity, which was 52.9% of revenue in 2021 and 47.8% in 2022 against 33.4% in 2025 and about 36% on the 2026 budget. The growth of the last three years has been unusually capital-light for this company precisely because it came from mix on installed capacity. That is the mechanism behind the margin expansion, and it is what changes from here.
  • First-half actuals of US$76.10bn plus the Q3 guide midpoint of US$45.2bn leave an implied Q4 2026 of US$50-53bn at "slightly above 40%" full-year growth, which is +11% to +16% sequentially and +49% to +56% year over year against a Q3 guide of +37% year over year
technology
  • The obvious technical candidates do not differentiate TSMC. Nanosheet gate-all-around is an industry-wide transition; Samsung shipped GAA at 3nm in 2022 and Intel shipped RibbonFET at 18A in 2025 [web]. ASML is the sole source of EUV and sells to Samsung and Intel as well.
  • What the evidence supports, in order of how much of the economics it explains, is yield learning and process control on nodes TSMC did not invent, advanced packaging capacity and reticle-area scaling, a design-enablement ecosystem funded by third parties, and cadence discipline.
  • A process node is a set of transistor geometry, interconnect and design rules that lets a customer put more switching elements into the same area at lower energy per switch.
  • Intel spent US$13.8bn on R&D in 2025, US$16.5bn in 2024 and US$16.0bn in 2023 against TSMC's NT$246,427m (US$7,921m) in 2025, and still writes in its own risk factors that it "has been unsuccessful to date in securing any significant external foundry customers for any of our nodes" (INTC 10-K FY2025).
  • PowerVia entered high-volume manufacturing with Intel 18A in late 2025 and Intel calls it "our unique industry-first implementation of backside power delivery" (INTC 10-K FY2025). TSMC's implementation, Super Power Rail, is reserved for A16 and guided for volume production in the second half of 2026. On this specific capability TSMC is the follower by roughly a year.
  • TSMC has deferred High-NA further than either leading-edge rival. At the 2026 North America symposium it signalled that A12 and A13 do not require High-NA, which means no High-NA requirement on any node through 2029 [web].
  • This is the franchise where management concedes the least defensible position, and it does so explicitly.
  • A competitor node with no certified flows, no PDKs and no ported IP is not a substitute even if its transistors are equal, which is why Intel's 10-K says potential customers "may be inclined to remain with their current foundry partners and unwilling to make the significant investments of time and resources needed to develop products on our nodes."
  • Six consecutive leading-edge nodes entered volume production between 2018 and 2025, each confirmed in the following annual filing.
value-chain
  • The step that matters is the one after manufacturing cost. Reported B200 street pricing is roughly US$30,000-40,000 [web]. TSMC's roughly US$2,000 of content is therefore 5.0-6.7% of what the end buyer pays.
  • packaging is roughly 55% of what TSMC collects per B200 (US$1,100 of US$2,000) against about 8% of consolidated revenue, because the consolidated figure is diluted by smartphone and mature-node revenue where packaging attach is low or zero.
  • The caveat is that this constraint is being relieved. CoWoS capacity moved from roughly 35,000 wafers per month at the end of 2024 to about 75,000 at the end of 2025, with 120,000-140,000 projected for the end of 2026, and TrendForce projects the supply-demand gap narrowing from about 20% to about 10% over 2026 [web].
  • TSMC has gained margin against its sole-source lithography supplier. The gross-margin gap, TSMC less ASML, was +4.8 points in FY2024, +7.1 points in FY2025 and +13.7 points on the most recent quarters.
  • Concentration is rising and management dismisses it: top-ten customers went from 70% to 78% of revenue over two years, and asked directly, C.C. Wei answered "No, that's not our concern... we are very happy about it" (Q2 2026 call).

TSMC manufactures integrated circuits to other companies' designs. It owns no product line of its own, sells no chip under its own brand, and competes for the manufacturing step between a customer's finished design and a packaged, tested die.

Company

earnings callsupports
Well, we always -- in the leading edge, we always live with fierce competition in the past. ... but one of our advantage is we do not compete with our customers and that plays a major role in us to earn the trust from our customers. But we don't take any competition lightly. We will just compete with them.
— Mark Liu (then Co-CEO & President), TSMC Q3 2017 earnings call, 2017-10-19
earnings callsupports
The foundry business model has proven to be the most efficient model in the semiconductor industry. As a pure-play dedicated foundry, we collaborate and work closely together with our customers to unleash their innovation to the market and enable their success. We do not have any internal products, and we do not compete with customers. Within foundry, TSMC competes on technology leadership, manufacturing excellence and customers' trust.
— C.C. Wei (then CEO), TSMC Q2 2019 earnings call, 2019-07-18
earnings callsupports
TSMC's mission is to be the trusted technology and capacity provider for the global logic IC industry for years to come. TSMC always treat our customers as partners. We do not compete with our customers. We grow our business by unleashing our customers' innovations and enabling their success. We earn our business by providing solid values, by providing industry-leading technologies, the world's largest logic capacity and efficient and cost-effective manufacturing to our customers, while maintaining trusting relationship with them.
— Mark Liu (then Chairman), TSMC Q2 2021 earnings call, 2021-07-15
filingsupports ✦ best
We manufacture a variety of semiconductors based on designs provided by our customers. Our business model is commonly called a “dedicated semiconductor foundry.” As a leader of the foundry segment, our net revenue and net income attributable to shareholders of the parent were NT$2,894,308 million and NT$1,158,380 million in 2024, and NT$3,809,054 million (US$121,423 million) and NT$1,697,604 million (US$54,116 million) in 2025, respectively. ... The principal source of our revenue is wafer fabrication, which accounted for approximately 86% of our net revenue in 2025. The rest of our net revenue was mainly derived from packaging and testing services, mask making, design, and royalty income.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16
filingsupports
TSMC's chief operating decision makers periodically review operating results, focusing on operating income generated by foundry segment. Operating results are used for resource allocation and/or performance assessment. As a result, the Company has only one operating segment, the foundry segment. The foundry segment engages mainly in the manufacturing, sales, packaging, testing and computer-aided design of integrated circuits and other semiconductor devices and the manufacturing of masks.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16
filingqualifies
Having one or more large customers that account for a significant percentage of our revenue may render us vulnerable to the loss of or significant curtailment of purchases by such customers ... While we generate revenue from hundreds of customers worldwide, our ten largest customers in 2023, 2024 and 2025 accounted for approximately, 70%, 76% and 78% of our net revenue in the respective year. Our largest customer in 2023, 2024 and 2025 accounted for 25%, 22% and 19% of our net revenue in the respective year. Our second largest customer in 2023, 2024 and 2025 accounted for 11%, 12%, and 17% of our net revenue in the respective year.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16
filingsupports
Because of our ability to provide a full array of services, we are able to accommodate customers with a variety of needs at every stage of the overall foundry process. The flexibility in input stages allows us to cater to a variety of customers with different in-house capabilities and thus to service a wider class of customers as compared to a foundry that cannot offer design or mask making services, for example.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16

These shifting business models could lead to significant variations in our sales if the growth of their products and services, particularly in the AI sector, is volatile or not sustainable.

TSMC deployed 305 distinct process technologies, and manufactured 12,682 products for 534 customers in 2025 by providing the broadest range of advanced, specialty and advanced packaging technology services.

filingqualifies

At present, nearly all of our Intel Foundry business supports internal manufacturing for Intel Products; however, we are offering our Intel Foundry services to external customers and aim to develop a more significant external foundry business in the future.

filingqualifies
An external foundry business is built on the principle of trust. We recognize that delivering high-quality, reliable and high-yield manufacturing services is essential to earning and retaining external foundry customers. Our foundry offerings include four components: wafer fabrication, advanced packaging, chiplet integration and design enablement services. ... We are actively seeking customers for our future Intel 14A node, which is our first node designed from inception as an offering to external customers.

Our primary competitor in leading-edge semiconductor process technology is TSMC, which holds a leading position in manufacturing at scale for the most advanced nodes. We also compete directly with Samsung in this market. Other Intel Foundry competitors include GlobalFoundries, UMC and SMIC, which primarily focus on mature process technologies.

filingsupports ✦ best
Pricing. We establish pricing levels for specific periods of time with our customers, some of which are subject to adjustment during the course of that period to take into account market conditions and other factors. We believe that customers find value in our flexible manufacturing capabilities, focus on customer service and timely delivery of high yield products, and this value is reflected in our pricing. Our pricing enables us to continue to invest significantly in research and development to deliver ever-improving products to our customers, and sustain healthy financial conditions to expand capacity and support customers' growth.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16
filingsupports
Advance Payment by Customers. Because of the fast-changing technology and functionality in semiconductor design, foundry customers generally do not place purchase orders far in advance to manufacture a particular type of product. However, some of our customers have entered into agreements with us to pay temporary receipts in order to retain specified capacity at our fabs. The treatment of advance temporary receipts, either by refund or by accounts receivable offsetting, will be determined by mutual consent when the terms and conditions set forth in the agreements are satisfied.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16

Peer: GlobalFoundries Inc.

filingcontradicts
We have entered into multiple LTAs with leading companies in the industry. Many of these contracts include customer advanced payments and capacity reservation fees in order to secure future supply. As of December 31, 2025, the aggregate remaining revenue commitment reflected by LTAs was approximately $11 billion. These LTAs include binding, multi-year, reciprocal minimum purchase and supply commitments with wafer pricing and associated mechanics outlined for the contract term.
— GlobalFoundries Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-02-27
filingsupports
Additionally, once our prices with a customer are negotiated, we are generally unable to revise pricing with that customer until our next regularly scheduled price adjustment. As a result, if market prices for essential components increase, we may be unable to pass the price increases through to our customers for products purchased under an existing agreement. Consequently, we are exposed to the risks associated with the volatility of prices for these components and our cost of revenue could increase and our gross margins could decrease in the event of price increases.
— GlobalFoundries Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-02-27
filingsupports
Product mix is among the most important factors affecting revenue and margins, as our wafer price varies significantly across technology platforms. The value of a wafer is determined principally by the uniqueness and complexity of the technology, performance characteristics, cost structure, yield and defect density. Devices with richer feature sets, higher performance, better yields and greater system-level integration require more substantial R&D investments and more complex manufacturing expertise and equipment, and thus generally command higher wafer prices.
— GlobalFoundries Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-02-27

— TrendForce: “TSMC Reportedly Raising N3P Prices 20% Ahead of 2nm Era, Pressuring Qualcomm and MediaTek”, 2025-09-23

Peer: United Microelectronics Corporation

filingsupports
We price our products on either a per die or a per wafer basis, taking into account a variety of factors, including the complexity of the technology, the prevailing market conditions, the order size, the cycle time, the strength and history of our relationship with the customer and our capacity utilization. Because semiconductor wafer prices tend to fluctuate frequently, we regularly review our pricing on a quarterly basis. ... Our average selling price decreased by 5.0% from 2023 to 2024 and decreased by 5.4% from 2024 to 2025, which reflects the nominal price erosion in 2024 and 2025.
— UMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-30
filingsupports
Our customers generally do not have long-term agreements with us to purchase wafers, and many customers do not place purchase orders far in advance, which makes it difficult for us to predict our future revenues, adjust production costs and allocate capacity efficiently on a timely basis. ... As a result, we do not typically operate with any significant backlog, except in periods of extreme capacity shortage.
— UMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-30

That five-year life sets the margin lifecycle of every node: a ramping node runs at a low margin and dilutes the consolidated figure, a node in its fourth year reaches the corporate average (management expects N3 to cross it in the second half of 2026, per the Q1 2026 call), and a node past five years runs at a margin the CFO describes as "generally very high" (Q1 2026 call).

filingsupports
Further, semiconductor manufacturing facilities require substantial investment to construct and are largely fixed cost assets once they are in operation. Because we own most of our manufacturing capacities, a significant portion of our operating costs is fixed. In general, these costs do not decline when customer demand or our capacity utilization rates drop, and thus declines in customer demand, among other factors, may significantly decrease our margins. Conversely, as product demand rises and factory utilization increases, the fixed costs are spread over increased output, which can improve our margins.
— TSMC Form 20-F, fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16

The leading-edge franchise holds a structural advantage of the strongest class available in manufacturing, and it is expensive to hold. The mature franchise earns excess returns today and is open to erosion. The backend franchise earns excess returns today and is the one part of the company where rivals are actively attacking the position.

filingsupports
We compete internationally and domestically with other foundry service providers, as well as with a number of integrated device manufacturers. We compete primarily on process technologies, manufacturing excellence, customer trust and service quality, such as earlier technology readiness, better quality, faster yield improvement and shorter cycle time. The level of competition varies with the process technologies involved. For example, in more mature technologies, competitors tend to be numerous and offer specialized processes. Some companies compete with us in selected geographic regions or niche application markets.
— TSMC Form 20-F FY2025, Item 4 Competition, 2026-04-16
sellsidesupports
It does not sit in the top band, which throws off excess returns without heavy reinvestment to defend — the attackers are well funded, state-supported, and closing the revenue gap, and TSMC must keep spending in Kumamoto and Dresden to hold position. It is also plainly not a no-edge, price-taking commodity business: a 15-35 point gross-margin premium sustained through a full downcycle is not what price-takers earn. It is an advantage that earns excess returns today, is defensible for now, and is open to erosion as Chinese capacity scales and as TSMC itself reallocates the asset base toward the leading edge.
— competitive-advantage agent (mature & specialty franchise), Deep-dive workpaper: Competitive advantage — Mature and specialty technologies (16nm and above), 2026-07-28
sellsidesupports
The 3DFabric backend earns excess returns today and is defensible for now, but it is open to erosion, and it is the one part of TSMC where the moat is being actively contested rather than merely admired. It sits at the middle of the three calibration points, not the top. The leading-edge wafer franchise inside the same company is the top-tier comparison; 3DFabric is visibly a tier below it, and the company's own disclosure is what shows this.
— competitive-advantage agent (3DFabric backend franchise), Deep-dive workpaper: Competitive advantage — Advanced packaging, testing, mask making and other services (3DFabric backend), 2026-07-28

Realistic case — Samsung's leading-edge portion at the midpoint of the band (US$4,500m), SMIC's at US$850m, Intel's external line at US$307m: 78,200 / (78,200 + 4,500 + 850 + 307) = 93.4%

sellsidesupports
First, the pricing-power test fails on management's own account. Advanced packaging has been in acknowledged shortage since mid-2023. Three years into that shortage, the CFO said in January 2026 that the margin is still below the corporate average. In July 2023, asked directly whether the CoWoS shortage was an opportunity to reprice, C.C. Wei answered that 'the most important thing for them right now is supply assurance.' A business that cannot reach its own company's average gross margin during a three-year shortage of its output is not exercising pricing power; it is choosing not to.
— competitive-advantage agent (3DFabric backend franchise), Deep-dive workpaper: Competitive advantage — Advanced packaging, testing, mask making and other services (3DFabric backend), 2026-07-28

filingsupports ✦ best

filingsupports

Our gross margin decreased from 32.6% in 2024 to 29.0% in 2025, primarily due to an annual decline of 5.4% in average selling price and a 2.9% appreciation of the NTD against the U.S. dollar in 2025 compared to 2024, partially offset by the increase in capacity utilization.

Every major government subsidises a rival: the US CHIPS Act, the European Chips Act (up to €5bn to ESMC), Japan's METI support for JASM and Rapidus, Korean support for Samsung. TSMC's own risk factor complains about unequal access to incentives. The consequence is quantified: overseas fabs dilute gross margin 2-3 percentage points in early stages, widening to 3-4 points later. This is the one moat dimension being actively eroded, and policy, not competition, is eroding it.

— Tower Semiconductor Form 20-F FY2025, Item 5 Operating and Financial Review — Revenue, 2026-03-11

Peer: US DOJ / FTC

webcontradicts
the agencies presume a merger to be illegal if it results in a post-merger HHI greater than 1,800, with a change greater than 100. ... Under the earlier standards, mergers warranted scrutiny in "moderately concentrated markets" with HHI between 1,500-2,500 and increases exceeding 100 points. In "highly concentrated markets" above 2,500 HHI, increases of 100-200 points drew scrutiny.
— Paul, Weiss client memo — DOJ and FTC Issue Final 2023 Merger Guidelines, 2023-12-20

Assuming every dollar of Samsung's, SMIC's and Intel Foundry's external revenue is leading-edge gives TSMC 77.2% of merchant sub-7nm revenue. On a realistic node split (Samsung's leading-edge portion at the midpoint of its band, SMIC's at US$850m) it is 93.4%. No assumption inside the plausible range moves it below three-quarters.

websupports ✦ best
Floor case — assume 100% of Samsung's, 100% of SMIC's, and 100% of Intel Foundry's external revenue is leading-edge: 78,200 / (78,200 + 12,800 + 10,000 + 307) = 77.2%. Realistic case — Samsung's leading-edge portion at the midpoint of the band (US$4,500m), SMIC's at US$850m, Intel's external line at US$307m: 78,200 / (78,200 + 4,500 + 850 + 307) = 93.4%
— Deep-dive workpaper: competitive-landscape — Leading-edge logic foundry (N7 and below), TSM run 2026-07-28, 2026-07-28
websupports
Both cases support the same conclusion: TSMC's share of merchant sub-7nm foundry revenue is somewhere between 77% and 93%, and no assumption within the plausible range moves it below three-quarters. The reason this differs from the widely-quoted ~72% "foundry market share" is denominator, not numerator.
— Deep-dive workpaper: competitive-landscape — Leading-edge logic foundry (N7 and below), TSM run 2026-07-28, 2026-07-28

TSMC does not trail on any leading-edge metric checked. The two places a rival holds an advantage are outside the franchise: Samsung's memory business earns a higher gross margin than TSMC's foundry, a fact TSMC's own chief executive volunteered, and Intel holds High-NA tools TSMC says it does not require through 2029.

Where TSMC trails. Nowhere in this franchise on the metrics checked. Peer: TrendForce (trade press)

webcontradicts
Although advanced packaging margins are currently below TSMC's overall average, they are expected to become a key profit driver as economies of scale expand. The average selling price (ASP) of a single CoWoS wafer is around $10,000 - on par with 7nm advanced process nodes. Advanced packaging has been steadily increasing its contribution, reaching about 10% in 2025.
— TSMC CoWoS Wafer ASP Reportedly Nears 7nm; Advanced Packaging to Become a Key Profit Driver, 2026-04-28

Under the Q2 2026 wafer mix, reproducing a 67.7% consolidated gross margin requires the N5/N7 band, both past the five-year machinery life, to run at 73-85%. Roughly 44% of wafer revenue is currently in that fully-depreciated band, down from 50% in FY2025 as N2 and N3 take share. That is a mechanical headwind nobody is modelling as one.

Two implications follow, both against the framing: the moat is stronger than the reported margin because the margin is capped by policy, not by competition, and the downside case in which TSMC "loses pricing power" is less dangerous than it appears because a large cushion sits between what TSMC charges and what the market would pay.

Every published long-term target has been raised after the realized number passed it, in every instance: revenue compound rate 5-10% (2018) to 10-15% (2021) to about 20% (Jan 2025) to about 25% (Jan 2026); gross margin 50% to 53% to 56%; return on equity above 25% to high-20s. Target-setting here is a lagging indicator of the company's own results, and the targets are a floor management is willing to be held to, not a central case.

Between the 2022 revenue peak and FY2025, TSMC shipped fewer wafers and earned 68% more revenue. Shipments went from 15,253 thousand 12-inch equivalents in 2022 to 15,022 thousand in 2025, a decline of 1.5%, while net revenue went from NT$2,263,891m to NT$3,809,054m. All of the three-year growth came from realized price and product mix; volume subtracted from it.

PowerVia entered high-volume manufacturing with Intel 18A in late 2025 and Intel calls it "our unique industry-first implementation of backside power delivery" (INTC 10-K FY2025). TSMC's implementation, Super Power Rail, is reserved for A16 and guided for volume production in the second half of 2026. On this specific capability TSMC is the follower by roughly a year.

Modern integrated circuit designers need sophisticated design infrastructure to optimize productivity and cycle time. Such infrastructure includes design flow for electronic design automation (“EDA”), silicon proven building blocks such as libraries and intellectual properties, simulation and verification design kits such as PDK and technology files. All of this infrastructure is built on top of the technology foundation, and each technology needs its own design infrastructure to be usable for designers. This is the concept of our technology platforms.
— TSMC Form 20-F for fiscal year 2025, 2026-04-16

As we pursue our strategy to establish Intel Foundry as a major provider of foundry capacity to manufacture semiconductors for others, we face intense competition from well-established competitors such as TSMC, Samsung, Global Foundries, UMC and SMIC. To succeed, we need to compete effectively across factors such as availability and time-to-market of manufacturing technology; advances in manufacturing processes in areas such as performance, performance per watt and density; multi-chip packaging; system integration; manufacturing capacity; price; margin; ease of use; quality; yields; customer satisfaction; and ecosystem support. ... Moreover, many of the largest potential foundry customers are fabless semiconductor companies whose products compete with our own. As a result, our strategy requires us to overcome customer concerns regarding protection of confidentiality information, intellectual property, and foundry capacity, among other competitive concerns, to attract and retain such customers.

We are not always successful or efficient in developing or implementing new process nodes and manufacturing processes. We experienced significant delays in implementing our 10nm process technology. In 2020, we encountered a defect mode in the development of our 7nm process technology that resulted in yield degradation, which was the primary driver for a delay in our expectations for our 7nm-based CPU product timing.
